I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES
(a) The More Important Representations made by
the Embassy in connection with the Sino-Japanese
Conflict.
711.9d
Pursuant to the Department's instruotions, the Ambassador
∴anded to the Minister for Foreign Affairs a formal note
dated February 4 calling his attention to depredations by
Japanese armed forces against American property in Soochow
and Hankow and to other numerous depredations of the same
kind. The note stated that the American Government found
it impossible to reconcile these lawless acts directed
against American mission properties with the assurances
repeatedly Eiven by the Japanese Government that American
rights and interests in China would be fully respected by
the armed forces of the Japanese Government; that it appeared
that these incidents had occurred with the knowledge, and some
of them in the presence, of Japanese officials, while other
actions, such as the occupancy of mission properties by Japanese
troops had presumably been taken by the express direction of
military officers; that arbitrary entry and occupation, together
with pilfering, looting and wanton destruction of property, coull
in o sense be regarded as acts of a character to b e expected
from a properly controlled military organization; and that the
American Government expected assuranccs from tho Japanese Govern-
ment that it would take iinmediate and specific steps to put an
end to the depredating of American properties and that it would
make full and complete indemnification for all losses and
damages inflicted.*
Making supplementary oral representations concerning these
acts of the Japanese military, Mr. Grew told Mr. Hirota that the
steadily mounting evidence of Japanese depredation would have ar
inflammable effect on the American public; that the patience of
the
Department's telegram No. 33, February 2, and Embassy's telegrm
No. 75, February 4, 1938).

The Embassy addressed an aide-memoire to the Foreign Ofrice
on February 7 stating that reports received by the American
Ambassador indicated that the Japanese euthorities at Shanghai
had not permitted the Chinese Customs to resume control over
the Japanese wharves; that there had thus arisen a condition
in which Japanese vessels putting into Shenghai had not been
paying the specified tonnage dues; and that cargo carried on
these Japenese vessels was being introduced into China without
the peyment of duty. The cide-mémoire pointed out that these
facts had been substantially admitted on February 3 by the
Jepanese Consul General at Shanghai, who had indicated that the
settlement of the matter of the Shanghai Customs would be a
condition precedent to the correction of the situation under
reference. Characterizing as indefensible the requirement that
observance of customs procedure t Shanghai be made contingent
upon acquiescence in Japanese wishes in regard to the settlement
of the Shanghai customs issue, the Embassy's aide-mémoire con-
cluded by requesting that the Japanese Government issue instruc-
tio..s to the Japanese authorities at Shanghai to permit resumption
of normal customs control over Japanese vessels cnd cargo.**

"I am instructed, however, to point out that
inder the circumstances which prevail end i: connection
with the areas described there rests upon American
officials and otler Anericen nationals in China no
obligation whatsoever tc tak? precautionary measures
requested on behalf of the Imperial forces in Your
Excellency's note under reference; I am to state
that toward safeguarding American lives and interests
involved precautionary measures heve voluntarily been
advised and voluntarily been taken in so far as
possible and that such measures will continue voluntarily
to be taken; but that, nevertheless, irrespective of
whether American nationals take or do not take such
precautionary mecsures, obligation rests upon the
Japanese military authorities to exert the utmost
precaution to the end that American nationals and property
shall not be injured by Japanese military operations;
end thet whether such requests of the Imperial forces
have or have not been complied with, whenever and
wherever merican nationals or property have been or
may be injured in consequence of the operations of
Japanese armed forces the American Government is
compelled to attribute to the Japanese Government
responsibility therefor.
American nationals and property lawfully located
in China possess there certain well-recognized rights
which are in no way altered by the circumstances of the
present conflict; American nationals, although advised
by American authorities to withdraw from areas in which
danger exists, are under no obligation to do so, and
in some cases find withdrawal impossible; merican
property situated in areas which are made or which are
about to be made theeters of military operations can
in most cases not be removed; this is obviously the
cese as regards real property; the presence of Chinese
military personnel or equipment in the neighborhood
of American nationals or property is clearly a circumstance
over which American officials or other imerican nationals
do not and cannot exercise control, and it in no way
alters the rights of such Americen nationcls or lessens
the obligations of the Japenese armed forces or authorities
to respect those rights; disclaimers of the responsibility
by the Imperial foroes will not alter these rights or
these obligations respectively. In the light of these
facts and considerations, failure on the part of American
officials or nationals to have complied with the requests
of the Imperial forces under the circumstances affords
no excuse for injury which has occurred or which may
occur to Amcrican netionals or property by Japanese armed
forces; and any such injury, as has been stated in my
note No. 781 of August 27, 1937, is considered by the
merican Government as upon the responsibility of the
Jepanese Government." *

(b) American Government's request for information
500A1505
concerning Japanese Naval Building Plans.
In accordance with the Department's instructions, on
February 5 the Embassy delivered to the Foreign Office a
note stating that under the London Naval Treaty of 1936 the
American Government was precluded from constructing capital
ships which exceed 35,000 tons or carry a gun of more than
16 inches or which are of less than 17,500 tons or carry
a gun of less than l0 inches and from constructing cruisers
in excess of 8,0o0 tons or with guns of more than 6 inches; that
for some time there had been persistent and cumulative reports
that Japan had undertaken or intended to undertake construction
cf capital ships and cruisers not in conformity with the above-
mentioned limits; that in the absence of explicit essurances
from the Japanese Government that these reports are ill-founded,
they must be deemed to be authentic; that the American Government
must exercise its riht of escalation under the London Naval
Treaty of 1936 unless the Japalese Government could furnish
these assurances and could satisfy the American Covernment that
it would not, prior to January 14, 1943, lay down capital ships
or acquire any vessels which do not conform to the limits in
question, without previously informing the American Government
of its intention to do so and of tonnage and caliber of the
largest gun of the vessel or vessels concerned; and that should
the

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.98)
-12
the Japanese Government be engaged in or intending to engage
in construction not in conformity with treaty limits and
should it be willirg to indicate the tonnages and calibers
of guns of the vessels in question. the American Government
would be ready to disouss with the Japanese Government the
question of the tonnages and gun calibers to be adhered to in
future if Japan were now prepared to agree to some limitation.*
The British and French Embassies delivered notes to the
Foreign Office that were identic with the American note ex-
cepting in minor particulors.
The Japanese press expressed resentment at the requests
of the three Fowers, characterizing such action as an arrogant
attempt to intimidate Japan and asserting that the United States,
Creat Britain, and France were simply tying to throw the burden
of naval armament expansion upon Japan.**
On February 12 the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs handed
o the Ambassador the Japanese Government's reply to the
Emb ssy's note of February 5. The substance of the Japanese
reply was as follows: As a result of the non-accoptance by other
couitries of the "reasonable desires" of Japan in the matter of
disurmament, there is as yet in existence no fair disarmament
treaty to which Japan is a party; in the opinion of the Japanese
Covernment, the mere communication of informetion concerning the
construction of vessels would not, in the absencc of quantitative
Jimitation, contributc to uny fair and equiteble measure of
disarmament; the Japanese Government is unable to comply with the
i sire of the Amcrican Government in the matter of communicating
information; the Japanese Government fails to sce any logical
roasoning in the American Government's assumption that the

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) The More Important Representations made by
the Embassy in connection with the Sino-Japanese
Confliot.
There was received on March 1 the reply of the Japanese
Foreign Office to the Embassy's note of December 1, 1037.
relating to the extraterritorial rights of Americans ir Manchuria.*
The reply stated in part as follows:
"The policy of the Government of Manchkuo in this
instance, which concerns the treatment of nationals of
third countries and juridical persons of third countries,
is a matter in which the Japanese Government is not
concerned and the Japanese Goverrment, accordingly, regrets
that it is not in a position to make any explanation."
In view of the Department's past instruction, no further
representations were made by the Embassy.**
In compliance with Department's telegraphic instructions
the Embassy addressed a memorandum to the Foreign Office conveying
the information received from the American Consul General at
Hankow to the effect that on February 28, 1938, Japanese airplanes
dropped six bombs close to the compound of the American Lutheran
Mission causing slight damage from concussion. The memorandum
further requested than an investigation be mede by the Japanese
authorities and that the Enbassy be advised of the results thereof
at the earliest possible moment. There was received n reply from
the Foreign Office dated March 16, 1938, enclosing a report from
the Japamose military authorities stating that in view of the
fact that the mission is located near the military objectives of
the air raid one of the bombs directed at the military objectives
may have fallen near the mission. This would seem to indicate
an admission on the part of the Japanese authorities of responsi-
bility for the damage which occurred in the instance under
reference.***

(d) Alaskan Fisheries.
Difficulties encountered before the completion of the
negotiations for the settlement of the question of selmon
fishing in Alaskan waters by Japanese subjects centered around
the wording of the press communique on that question to be
issued by both Governments.
The Japanese Government insisted upon avoiding any
implication of an admittance on its part that Japanese subjects
had fished for salmon in Alaskan waters. They further insisted
that the temporary character of the Jepanese Government's
assurances be made more cvident. The Embassy in its negotiations
with the Foreign Office repeetedly emphasized the fact that any
substantial departure from the Department's original draft of
the press communique would not have the effect of alleying
public feeling in the United States and that any stress on the
temporary character of the Japanese assurances to refrain from
issuing licenses for salmon fishing in Bristol Bay would serious-
ly injure the purpose and result of the press communique in the
United States.
On March 24 the final redraft prepared in negotiations
between the Embassy with the Foreign Office was decided upon
and telegraphed to the Department. Approval was received from
the Department on March 25, and the communique was released for
publication on that date.
The press communique was as follows:
"As a result of discussions between the merican Governm.
and Government of Japan in rogard to the snlnon fishing
activities
*
Despatch No. 2811, March 17, 1938.

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
activities of Japanese nationals in the off shore
waters of Alaska, especially in tho Bristol Bay
area, reported during the past fishing season.
the Japanese Government hes given without prejudice
to the question of rights under internntional law,
assurances as follows:
(1) That the Japanose Governnent is suspending
the three year salmon fishing survey which has been
in progress since 1936 in the waters in question;
(2) That in as much as salmon fishing by Japanese
vessels is not permitted without licenses from the
Jepanese Governnent and as tho Government has been
refraining from issuing such licenses to those
vessels which desired to proceed to the Bristol Bay
aren to fish for salmon, it will, on its own initiative,
continue to suspend the issuance of such licenses;
that in order to make effective this assurance the
Japanese Government is prepared to take, if and when
conclusive evidence is presented that any Japanese
vessels engagege in salmon fishing on a conmercial
scale in waters in question, necessary and proper
measures to prevent any such further operations.
Tho American Government nppreciates these
assurances which the Japanese Government has given
in the spirit of collaboration in the efforts of the
American Government to conserve and protect the
Alaskan salmon fishery resources and is gratified
that discussions have been conducted by the two
Governments concerned in a friendly manner.
In view of the above assurances it is evident
that if over Japanese vessels, which were present
in the wuters in question to engage in crab fishing
or in production of fish meal, caught salmon in
commercial quantities in the past, such fishing
was conducted without the knowledge of the Japanese
Government.
Furthermoro these nssurances of the Japanese
Government are regarded as regulating the situntion
until such time as the problems involved i. y call
for, and circunstances r y render practicable, the
taking of other mensures."
Release of the above communiquc in full was not given
in Japan.

(e)
Payment by American Citizens of Special Japanese Taxes
for Military Expenditures in China.
On March 5 a note verbale setting forth the views of the
Japanese Government with regard to the payment of Special Japanese
Taxes for military expenditures by American citizens was
received from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in reply to the
Fmbassy's aide-mémoire of February 21, 1938. This reply stated
in essence that the special taxes under reference have the
character of general sources of revenue, which character is
not altered by reason of their payment into the Extraordinary
Military Expenditures Special Account; that convenience in
balancing the accounts of the Government alone dictated this
method of accounting and in no way altered the character of
the taxes; that the term "military exactions or contributions"
of paragraph four, article 1, of the Treaty of Commerce and
Navigation cf l9ll should be defined as meaning military
requisitions

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
requisitions or military contributions enforced by the
military authorities, and is therefore not applicable to
taxes set up by normal legislative processes; that American
citizens cannot escape from the obligation imposed upon them
by article two of the above-mentioned treaty; that the Japanese
Government cannot discontinue efforts to collect these speoial
taxes from American citizens. The reply cited the example of
the law passed in 1904 in connection with the Russo-Japanese
War to which the British Government made no objection to the
payment of the taxes imposed on its nationals under similar
circumstances.
On March 23, 1938, and under authorization of the Depart-
ment, the Ambassador again addressed a note to the Foreign
Office restating the views of the American Government and
emphasizing two points. (1) The proceeds from the taxes are
paid into and disbursed from a special account for military
purposes. It was clearly pointed out that the law in question
provides thet all military expenditures connected with the North
China Incident shall be paid from this specinl account, but does
not provide for the payment of goneral administrative expendi-
tures from this nccount. It is therefore obvious that the
military operations are supported by funds into which the
special taxes are paid and that these taxes re used only for
that purpose. (2) The Americen Government holds the view that
any special taxes which are imposed expressly for the support of
military operations in Chinn are "military exactions or contri-
butions" from which American citizens are exempt under the
provisions of the Trenty of Commerce and Navigation of 19ll.
In further stressing this point, the Ambassador's note brought
to the attention of the Jpanese Government the fact that i
its

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.99)
10
its interpretation of the menning of the term in question were
valid, it would annul the obvious intentions of the Japanese and
American Governments, which was to prevent levies for military
purposes and not merely to proscribe one method by which such
levies might be collected,
The Japanese Government was also informed that, in the
present instance, the views of the British Government coincide
with those of the American Government.*
With regard to the securing of foreign capitnl for the
Manchuria Industrial Development Corporation it wes reported on
March 5 that in answer to a question posed by a member of the
House of Representatives budget committee the War Minister
replied thnt the United States is not the only country being
considered and that they(the military authorities) are well
awarc of possible difficulties rising from the solicitation of
foreign cepital. It wcs further reported thnt in reply to the
question anes the Nar Minister think anyone would invest in a
stock in which he is deprived of c swaying vote?", the War
Minister requested that he be allowed to reply in a closed
session. The public was then excluded and the committee remained
in camera for nearly an hour before adjourning.
Remarks of the Foreign Minister, Mr. Koki Hirota pertinent
to relation of the United Stntes mnde in the Lower House of

to the United States by press representatives. The press version
of the remarks reed in part as follows: ".......it is, I think,
a matter that should make us feel sorry for the United States."
he
*Imbassy's Despatch No. 284l, March 29, 1938, and Department's
telegram No. 98, Mnrch 19, 3 p.m.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)

The effect of press despatches prior to the enactment of the

(g)
Return of "Peoplus Envoys"
During the month of March several of the "peoples envoys"
returned to Japan from abroad. Those returning from the United
States were unanimous in stating that they found the attitude
of the United States to be unsympathetic towards Japan. In
explaining this attitude emphasis was pl&ced on the ignorance
of Americans, even of those in "high positions", of conditions
in the Far East nnd also on the traditional sympathy of the
United States for the "under dog".
II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(n)
China.
(1) Military Operations.
The War Minister was reported to have steted at a meeting
of the House of Representatives budget committee on March 4 as
follows:
*Embassy's Despatch No. 2808, Morch 15, 1938.

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
follows: "In preparing for prolonged hostilities, ample
fighting power must be maintained, cnd the transfer homeward
of the troops at the front in exchange for home troops going
to the front will be necessery, I think. As for the methods
involved, I cannot make ny statement for strntegic reasons".
This statement will servo to explain the difficulty of neutral
observers in Japan attempting to make accurate estimate of the
net movement of Japanese troops from Japan to China.
Military operations in China during the month of March
continued in prectically the same theatres as during February.
Operations in Shansi Province were rapidly pushed forward
to the Yellow River on the west and south boundaries of that
province. Progress was so rapid that the province was not
cleared of Chinese forces, and isolated groups remained active
within tho province causing the Japanese forces to make "zigzag"
manocuvres in pursuit of these guerilla bands. Chinese forces
in small numbers enterod the northern part of the province when
the Japanese "push" was started to the south towards the
Yellow River. At the close of the month, in spite of the
Japanese front line being extendod to the Yellow River, fighting
with guerilla bands within the province was occupying considerable
attention of the Japanese army.
In Shantung province the Japanese continued their attempt
to push southward towards Hsuchow. In this general area the
Chinese succeeded in holding the Japanese forces along the
Grand Canal. Ichow which has long been a Japanese objective
was not taken. Severe fighting occurred north of Hsuchow with
little or no chnnges resulting. A movement north from Nanking
toward

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
toward Hsuohow in conjunction with pressure being exerted
southward from Shantung is an attompt to use forces on both
sides of the Chinese to reach this objective.
Repreated attacks were made by the Chinese along the Hwni
Ho River northwest of Nanking. These cttacks hindered the
Japanese advances towards Suchow.
Further small advances were made by the Japanese northward
from the Yangtze River in Kiangsu Province.
A force of some 30,O00 Chinese was reported in the area
between Nanking and Tnihu Lake. This area had formally been
cleared of Chinese forces by the Jopanese in their advance on
Nanking.
The Chinese guerilla warfare continued throughout most of
the aren occupied by the Japanese. Little cctual military
advantnge was gained from these operations by the Chinese; it
does however offer an offective strategem in deleying the
Japanese and prolonging hostilities.
(2)
Aerial Operations.
Aerial operations by the Japanese continued along the same
strategic plan which has been in operation for some months.
Army plenes were used largely in support of front line troop
operations and attack on front line objectives. Naval planes
were used principally for long range bombing of railways,
particularly the Canton-Henkow line and the Canton-Kowloon line.
Raids were also made on strategic centres in the interior of
Chinn such as Hankow and Nancheng. It is difficult and of
doubtful value to attempt an accurate report of the results of
these long renge bombings. Some resistance was shown by the Chinese
airforces reportedly owing to the presence of new Soviet planes
and pilots.
Japanese

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
Japanese press continued to report rather fabulous
success and almost divine invulnerability for their own
air forces.
(3) Autonomous Régimes under Japanese Patronage.
During the month of March various new régimes were
established in areas in China under the control of the Japanese
military forces. Particularly noteworthy was the inauguration
on March 28 of the new administration for central China,
called the "Renovation Government of the Chinese Republic".
the new régime was installcd under pntronage of the Japanese
military forces and congratulatory messages were sent by both
General Hata on behalf of the Japanose army and Vice-Admiral
Hasegawa for the Japanese navy. Close cooperation with the
Provisional Government of the Chinese Republic at Peiping and
with "friendly powers" in establishing permanent peace in the
Orient and opposing communism nd the Kuomintang régime was
plcdged in the menifesto of the new Government. At the same
time small local autonomous Governments in central China pledged
cooperation with the new Government at Nanking.
The new Government in Nanking stated that recognition would
not be accorded to any treaty or contract concluded after 1927
or hereafter between a power or powers'and the former Nanking
Government. Assurances were given that legitimate rights and
interests of foreign Governments nd nationals in China will be
duly respected in accordance with the principles of international
law end usage. The inauguration was widely heiled in the Japanese
press.
On March 5, Mr. Mc Liang, former president of Peking Univer-

Japan is more necessary than anything else, it seems to us".

There

incidents involving the two countries. In the Japanese press,
there ware no evidences of an organized effort on the part of

erticles during the month concerning the prolonged tension between
the Soviet Union and Japan. Relations with Russin were the subject
of interpelletions in the Diet which were answered calmly by the
Government.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL CONDITIONS
(a) The Cabinet and the Political Parties.
There continued to be some friction between the Governaent
and the political parties. An attack on Mr. Isoo Abe, leader of
the Social Mass Party, by two ruffians, was the subject of a
vigorous interpellation in the Diet. In reply, Premier Konoe
promised to redouble protective measures for Diet rembers. The
Minseito and Seiyukai pnrties refused to cooperate with the
Social

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.99)
-27-
Social Mass party in sponsoring an impeachment of the Home
Minister. The atmosphere in Tokyo was particularly tense at
this time, but with the arrest of the ruffians as well as some
of those reportedly backing the attack, and the lack of
vigorous action in the Diet, tension eased and the cabinet con-
tinued its efforts to obtain approval of its legislation.
Rumors of the new single state party continued to be
prevalent, and on March 10 it was reported that Premier Konoe
had at last agreed to lead such a party. The incident in which
Lieutenant-Colonel Masaoki Sato told a Diet member to "shut up"
during a committee hearing on the mobilization bill had reportedly
revealed to the Prime Minister the friction between the Government
and the parties and had shown clearly the need of one national
party. It seemed apparent that the Seiyukai was attempting to
push the program, but with the uncertainty shown by the Minseito,
nothing evolved and the Diet closed without a new party being
organized. The possibility that such a party may yet be formed
remains a powerful threat against recalcitrants. On March 28 at
a meeting of "right wing" groups, particularly the Tohokai, a
strong resolution urging the necessity of one party was adopted,
but at the same time it seemed apparent that the two large
parties would continue to support the Konoe Cabinet and to
cooperate with each other without amalgamation. This movement
is one which may be brought to a head at any moment believed to
be propitious for the realization of the aims of those who even
now control governmental policies.
In spite of the support of Mr. Yukio Ozaki, the eminent
liberal, who plead for the principle of free speech, Mr. Suehiro
Mishio of the Social Mass Party was expelled from the Diet for
daring to venture the suggestion that Fremier Konoe should proceed
with

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.98)
-28-
with the boldness and determination of Stalin, along with
Mussolini and Hitler. The mention of Stalin was believed to
betray sympathy toward communism and could not be allowed to
stand without disciplinary action.
(b)
Administrative Decisions and Proposed Reorganization.
To eliminate the lack of coordination between the various
officials and official institutions responsible for Japanese
management of affairs in China, the Legislative Bureau and the
Foreign Office prepered divergent plans. The Cabinet in
commenting on these two plans decided on March 15 to create the
China Economic Affnirs Bureau to be placed under the direction
of the Cabinet. The Legislative Burenu had originally proposed
to establish a board under the direction of the Premier to be
entrusted with political, economical and cultural matters
relating to Chine and to be independent of the Foreign Office.
Foreign Minister Hirota proposed to establish a bureau under
the direction of the Ministry for Foreign ffairs to handle only
FP
the economic exploitation of China; political and cultural
matters were to be controlled as hitherto by the Foreign Office.
Under the plan referred to above as adopted by the Cabinet, there
will be established a China Economic Affairs Burenu which will
have no voico in diplomatic matters and the principal duty of
it will be to supervise the North Chino Development Compeny and
the Central China Rohabilitation Company.
The explanation has been advanced thet rather than because
of his fear that an independent bonrd, such as suggested by the
Legislntive Bureau's plan, might be accused of infringing upon
China's sovereignty and hence be displeasing to third countries,
Forcign Minister Hirota opposed that plan because he is lonth
'o relinquish power which he had wielded up to the present and
o hand over the direction of affairs in Chinn to a board which migh
take

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.98)
-29-
take a more moderate view toward the Chinese than that
assumed by the Foreign Office itself.*

TY. RSE OF LEGYCLATTON
The seventy-third session of the Diet was formally closed
on March 27, 1938, having finished its business th rrevious
day with the passage of the last of the Government's legislation,
the electric power bill. This session, in spite of the heated
debates and vigorous questioning in connittee sessions, marked
a complete triumph for the Governmont and resulted in the
enchancement of Prince Konoe's position and popularity.
In the National Mobilization bill, the Diet relinquished
all power to the Cabinet, to be exercised through mperial decree
in time of war or incidents of the nature of war, or when there
is the necessity for n:tional mobilization.*
By the electric power bill the Government is to b) given
control of the industry through a state-controlled clectric
power company to which private interests must transfor their
frcilities as an investment in that company.
Two other bills worthy of particular note were those
providing for the establishment of government controlled
companies for the economic development and exploitation of areas
occupied by the Japanese military forces in North nnd Contral
hina.
The reports concorning the North Chinn Industrial Develop-
nt Company were the cause of the British Embassy delivering
momorandum to the Foreign Office on the subject.**
The other
unpany is to be the Central China Development Company.

It was announced in the local press on March 9, 1938, that
the House of Representatives had approved six financial bills,
including the ¥4,850,000,000 supplementnry budget for the
military operations in China, ¥21,800,000 and ¥386,492,000
supplementary budgets for the general accounts for the current
and coming fiscal years, and the supplementery budgets for the
special accounts for the current and coming fiscal years.
On Merch 12 the approval of the House of Peers to the
supplementary budget for operations in Chinn was announced.
• This
provided for ¥3,257,000,000 for the army, ¥1,043,000,000 for the

SECTION VI. GENERAL ECONOM
AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
(a)
Summary of the Economic Situation for the Month of
The gencral economic situation in Japan during the month
under review clearly reflected the strengthening of Government
control and regulation of all economic cndeavors. As regards
those industries connected with the manufacture of ammunition
and war materials in general, the condition was one approaching
a "boom period"; Government expenditures and subsidies were
relatively
large and growing, wages were /high and the munitions industrys
only difficulty was its inability to make sufficiently rapid
expansion. Other undertakings, however, were adversely effected
by the decline of foreign trede and by the Government's control
policies and emphnsis on the necessity of general economizing.
Foreign exchnnge was well stabilized by the Government, bank
deposits increased, the money market was easy and capital pay-
ment were lerge. It is expocted that Government revenues for
the month under higher rntes of taxation will continue to
show large increases over last year. There was a continued
slight inflationary trend and the currency issue was somewhat
further expanded. The commodity price level continued to rise,
The sales of national bond issues were substantially larger than
last year.
(b) Foreign Trade.
Lergely owing to the Government's expansion policy along
with
Statistical data obtained from Commercial Attaché's Office

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
-33-
relatively
with the high wages of the munitions industries and similar
enterprises, manufacturing costs in Japan showed a continuing
rise. This tends to rob Japanese commodities of one of their
principal advantages in the world market and was reflected in
a continuation of the declining trend of the foreign trade of
Japan.
The Government's policy of rigid import and export con-
trol was also a definite factor in this decline in that the
supply of raw materials was limited. Total exports for the
month dropped by 18% and imports by 39% from March, 1937 levels.
Exports of raw silk, silk, rayon and cotton textiles, end tinned
foods all declined, while imports of such commodities as wool,
raw cotton, pulp, rubber and leather also fell off. Excess
imports for the month was ¥18,557,000 against ¥124,683,000 for
March of last year.

(d) Money Market.
In relation to the same month last year interest for short

1.095% to 2.555% per nnnum. The total benk deposits at the
end of the month were ¥16,005,161,000, which is an increase
of ¥253,567,000 over the month of February and an increase of
¥1,700,449,000 over the corresponding period of last year. This
increase mey be accounted for by the large Government expenditures
and the Government policy of regulating investments. Postal
savings, which ordinarily show a decline during the month of
March, continued to gain during Mrrch 1938, probably owing to
the official campaign for economizing by the people.

apital payments during the month were ¥672,721,150, an increase

largely

(r) Commodity Prices.
As has been the trend ror some time,commodity prices
continued to advence during the month of March. The Bank of
Japan's (1927=lo0) wholesale price index number for March was
251.5 compared with 249.3 in February, 1938, and 239.8 in
March 1937. Retail prices conformed; their index number as of
April 15 (index numbers are available only for the preceding
month) was 197.6 compared with 192.4 on March 15 and 171.9 on
April 15 of 1937.
It was announced on March 2, 1938 that on May 1 a
rationing system for consumption of oil and gasoline would be
introduced. All purchases will be controlled by a ticket system
and severe punishment is provided for violations. Further
restrictions on the free use of other commodities mey be expected
as the need for consorving them is felt.

IX. IMMIGRATION.
It was reported on March 16 that the Overseas Ministry had
requested somc 5,570,000 Yen for the purpose of financing mass
:mmigretion movement of about 1,500,000 youths to "Manchukuo"
over e period of 20 years beginning on April 1 of this year. It
i: planned to send 30,000 during 1938 - this in addition to the
 yenr plan now in effect which is to move one million families
io the new country. The youths are to be unmarried and between
ti ages of 16 and 19. They will be trained in a Central Training
ustitute in Ibaraki for two months before going to Manchuria

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
-36-
and will be given actual practico in collective farming in
North Manchuria in some five training camps. Recruiting for
this movement will be undertaken by the Overseas Ministry,
prefectural governmonts, the Manchurian Immigration Socicty and
youth associations. In Manchuria the Manchuria Development
Corporation and the Manchuria Government will supervise the
immigrants. A yearly subsidy of ¥3,000,000 will be granted by
these two orgnnizations.

opernted in regular pessenger service on the Pacific. Outstanding

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
The More Important Representations made by the
Embassy in connection with the Sino-Japanese
Conflict.
In accordance with the Department's instructions, the
p
Ambassador called on the Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs on
April 4 and made strong representations concerning the return
of American citizens to Nanking, urging that Americans who
had interests there be permitted to procceed to and remain
in that city, as Japanese nationals appeared to be doing in
considerable numbers. These representations were supported by
an aide-mémoire and a soparate informal document which presented
certain facts described in a telegram from the Embassy at Nanking.*
On April 5 the Ambassador received from the Minister for
Foreign Affairs a note dated April 2 in reply to his representa-
tions of February 4, 1938, with respect to the looting of and
other deprodations on American property by Japanose forces in
China. The substarce of the Japanese Government's reply was that
Jupanese officials in China were erdeavoring to ascertain the
fects concerning the roported depredations and that the Imperial
Government wns prepared to puy approprinte indemnification in those
cases where, as a result of such investigations, definite evidence
was obtained that injury was improperly caused by Japanese forces.
In conclusion, Mr. Hirota's not: stated that until investigation
or the ectual circumstences should make available ovidunce of the
acts, the Japanese Government could not concur with the following
conclusion or the American Government, as contained in the Ambassa-
or's ncte of February 4, "It appears that these incidents have
ccurred with the knowledge, and some indeed in the presence, of
cpanese orficials, while others, such as the occupancy of mission
property
Department's telegram No. 116, April 1, Embassy's telegram No.
225, April 4, end telegram from the Embassy at Nankine No. 90,
April. i.

direction of military officers".*

(h) Australia.
On May 19 Prime Minister Lyons declared before the Australian
Parliment his intention of prohibiting the exportation of iron
ore from Australia as of July 1, 1938. The spokesman of the
Japanese Foreign Office issued en official statement on May 24
to the effect that Japan wished reconsideration by the Australian
Government in this matter; that iron ore was a staple import of
Japan from Australia; that the Yompi Sound Iron Mine, where much
Japanese capital was invested, was being exploited under an
explicit agreement with the Provincial Australian Government; and
that, therefore, tho step announced by the Austrelian Prime
Minister "must be said to be lacking in faith". According to
the NICHI NICHI of May 25, the Foreign Office spokesman's announce-
ment contained the statement that the measure proposed by the
Canberra Government might aggrevate Australian-Jepanese relations,
which had been taking a favorable turn.**

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) The More Important Representations made by the Embassy
in connection with the Sino-Japanese Conflict.
711.94
It will be remembered that on May 31 the Ambassador delivered
(11
to the Vice-Minister for Foreign Affairs a signed note addressed
to the Minister for Foreign Affairs protesting against the continued
occupation by the Japanese military of American property in China,
with the consequent inability of American citizens to reenter their
properties.* The Foreign Office informed the Embassy on June 2 that
a committee consisting of Colonel Nishi of the General Staff,
Commander Kami of the Navy Department, Mr. Ishii of the American
Affairs Bureau of the Foreign Office, and one secretary from the
East Asia Bureau of the Foreign Office, would be sent to China to
investigate the conditions against which the Embassy had protested
and also to formulate means for satisfactory adjustment of such
conditions.**
On June 3 the Embassy's Naval Attaché discussed with the Senior
Aide to the Navy Minister the attacks by Japanese on May 19 on the
Chinese personnel aboard the American launches "TEXACO ONE" and
"DAHLAY" at Shanghai, concerning which the Ambassador had made
vigorous written representations on May 31.*** After attempting to
explain the reasons for the attacks, the Senior Aide requested
Captain Bemis to transmit his explanation to the United States Navy
Department in order that it might be realized in the United States
that such actions of Japanese naval personnel were not directed
against American rights and interests. It was evident to the
Embassy's Naval Attaché that the Senior Aide was gravely concerned
bout this matter.****

On the basis of a telegraphic report from the American
Consulate at Tsingtao* , the Ambassador called on the Vice-Minister
for Foreign Affairs on June 9 and made strong oral representatiors,
supported by a note, against the shooting and wounding by a
drunken Japanese soldier at Tsining on June 2 of Doctor Frederick
G. Scovel, an American Presbyterian missionary who was said to
be attempting to protect Chinese nurses in his mission hospital
against Japanese soldiers who were looking for women. In reply
to the Ambassador's representations, Mr. Horinouchi stated that
the Japanese military authorities took a serious view of the
matter and that as a result of representations made by the American
Government's representatives in Peiping end Tsingtno those authori-
ties were making a careful investigation.** A Secretary of the
Japanese Embassy called ut the American Embassy in Peiping on June
20 and read from a document written by the Japanese military
authorities an expression of regret on the part of the latter for
the wounding of Dr. Scovel. The document placcd the bleme on the
"drunken" and "undesirable" Japanose soldier end stated that he
would be punished severely in accordance with the Army's laws
and regulations.***
On the occasion of Mr. Grew's call on Mr. Horinouchi on
June 9 in connection with the Scovel case, the Ambassador said
that he wished to speak informally with reg rd to the bombing
by Japanese forcos of civilian populations in China; that hc had
not received instructions to make official reprosentations and was
rot muking such representations; and that he was acting on his
own initiative and from the point of view of Japsnese-imerican
relations.

hostilities. Basing its position largely on the interpretation

in the Treaty of Commerce between Japan and the United States,
as meaning forcible levies made during an emergency by the
military authorities themselves, the Foreign Office stated that it
wes unable to withdraw its contontion in regerd to the levying of
such taxes.*

to one yen) and transferred to the Yokohame Specie Bank in the
United Stetes; that these arounts would be held in this bank in
dollar balances, without interest, for three years, at the ccnclu-
sion of which period they would be payable to the respective
American companies. The Emtassy's inforaant stated that he believed
that the Japanese Government had decided to accept this proposal,
and that fcrmal approval was sinply a ratter of time and of certain
fornalities.*

MISCELLANEOUS.
Accorcing to Government statistics published during the month,
the population of Japan increased during the year 1937 by 972,510,
there having Been 2,180,589 births nnd 1,208,079 deaths.
In "Weekly Gazette" No. 86 issued by the Infcrmation Bureau of
the Cabinet, the Department of Justice published an article reporting
that since the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese conflict the majority
of the 60,000 "ex-cormunists" in Japan had voluntarily made efforts
to understand the significance of the conflict and had shown an
attitude of sacrifice for the sake of the country; that such a change
of mind was not a sudden phenomenon but was apparently the result
of "arduous guidance and protecticn y tho authorities" during the
past few years; and that the enforcenent in Ccteber 1936 of the
"Thought Offenders' Protection and Observation Law" had preved
effective in carrying out this "guiance".

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 21, 1938.
MOH
The following material in Tokyo's political
report for August may be of interest:
Page 15. It was reported to the Japanese
press on August 6 that the South Manchurian
Railway will assume control of all coal mining
and coal marketing in Shantung Province.
Page 15. It was announced in the Japanese
press during the month that the East Asia Agri-
cultural and Forestry Council had adopted during
its sessions at the official residence of the
Japanese Minister for Agriculture and Forestry
an agrarian policy for Japan and the occupied
areas in China, including plans for rice, wheat,
cattle raising, forestry, and sericulture and
covering all the areas under Japanese domination
from Karafuto to Central China.
Page 23, (a). Rumors of a single-party
movement came to the fore again in August.
Page 24, (c). In the course of discussion
on the "China Organ" during the month of August,
divergences had developed between the Military
and the Ministries of Foreign Affairs and
Finance, but there were indications that every
effort would be made to come to a decision
before the fall of Hankow.

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
Foreign Affairs concerning American Desiderata
in Connection with the Sino-Japanese Hostilities.
In a telegram dated June 22* the Ambassador informed the
Department that he intended to have an interview with the Minister
for Foreign Affairs, recommended to the Department that he be
authorized to take up with General Ugaki in the proposed interview
certain pending questions affecting American rights and interests
in China, and requested the Department's instructions concerning
the specific issues which should be brought forth in such a dis-
cussion. The Department approved the interview proposed by the
PP1-11
Ambassador and set forth certain desiderate of the American Govern-
ment in connection with the Sino-Japanese hostilities**. According-
ly, on July 4 Mr. Grew called on General Ugaki and had a conversa-
tion with him which lasted more than two hours.
Before the Ambassador made his representations, the Minister
for Foreign Affairs brogched the following topics: the desirability
of having the American authorities in China prevent the military
utilization by the Chinese of American property; articles in
merican magazines disrespectful to the Japaneso Imperial Household;
disadvantages of the giving of official American releases to the
press concerning incidents in process of settlement; end the recent
statement by the Japanese spokesman in Shanghai concerning the
exercise by third perty nationals of extraterritorial rights***.
Following General Ugaki's observations, Mr. Grew proceeded with
his own representations under the following headings: (1). Respect
for American property rights; (2). Maintenance of the personal
rights of American citizens; (3). Maintenance of equality of
opportunity in Japanese controlled areas in China as between Japanes
and others; (4). Protection of legitimate American financial
interests
*
Embassy's telegram No. 399, June 22, 1938.
**
Department's telegram No. 212, June 25, 1938.
***
Embassy's despatch No. 3081, July 8, 1938, and Embassy's
telegram No. 448, July 7, 1938.

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
interests; (5). Avoidance of interference with American treaty
and presoriptive rights in China, including extraterritorial
rights in general and administrative rights in the International
Sottlement at Shanghai; (6). The desirability of General Ugaki's
using his influence to ensure the above-mentioned desiderata in
the interests of American-Japanese friendship; and (7). General
principles*. The last item, general principles, included certain
broad interests and concerns of the American Governnent and people
as expressed in the Secretary's public statements of July 18 and
August 23, 1937, and in his speeches at the National Press Club
in Washington on March 17, 1938, and before the annual meeting of
the Ber Association of Tennessee at Nashville on June 3, 1938.
Mr. Grew's representations pointed out that Mr. Hull's statements
and speeches indicated that the existence and circumstances of the
conflict in China were deplored by the Americen people, who had
become increasingly perturbed by the methods employed by Japan

strict impartiality; that no nation and no people cculd escape the

but also on grcunds of the menace to American life and property the
widespread bombing of civilian populations in China had profoundly
shocked both the American Government and the American people; and
thet the American Government was most deeply anxious that the
conflict be concluded and that peace be restored at the earliest
possible moment, with due regard for the establishment and main-
tenance of orderly procosses in the relations of nations along
lines consistent with tho provisions of existing international
commitments and consistent with principles of justice and equity

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a) China.
(1)
Military Operations.
Continuing their drive in the fangtze area toward Hankow,
the Chinese capital, the Japanese forces occupied Hukow, about
30
miles below Kiukiang, on July 5 and three weeks later succeeded
in taking possession of Kiukiang. A communiqué issued by the
793.94
press section of the Japanese fleet in China on July 26 stated
that Kiukiang, the principel town of strategic inportance for the
defense of Hankow, had been taken as a result of joint operations
of the Army and Navy. According to an announcement made on July
28 by the headquarters of the Japanese Army in Shanghai, follow-
ing their capture of Kiukiang Japanese military and naval forces
resuned their offensive in the Yangtze Valley, occupying Taihu
in scuthwestern Anhwei Province west of Anking on July 27, and
Eaining victories cver Chinese troops nidway between Kiukiang and
Nanchang.
Meanwhile, Japanese forces roportodly started an offensive
on July 5 against the Chinese trcops,estimated at 300,000, entrench-
ed in Southern Shansi Province. According to Japanese news reports,
Japanese troops were successful in rescuing Japanese units that
had been besieged in Southern Shansi for two months and in routing
the bulk of the Chinese forces remaining in that district. It was
also reported that Japanese forces had cornenced "nopping up"
operations against enemy forces romaining in Hopei, Shantung, and
Honan Provinces.

On July 1 General Itagaki, the War Minister, and Mr. Ikeda,

Energency

Imergenoy", the War Minister declared before a mass neeting in
Tokyo on July 7 that there had newer been in world history such
glorious victories as those just achieved by the Japanese Army;
that Japan had not been fighting the Chinese people but against
the Communists and the anti-Japanese policy of the Chiang Kai-shek

in fact a greater war than the Sino-Japanese War of 1894-5 or the

A spokesman of the Imperial Headquarters announced on July 6

Army had lost 36,629 men and the Navy "more than" 1,100 men, while
China was estimated to have lost 1,300,000 men, of whom 510,009
dead had been counted on battlefields.
Mr. Akira Kazam?, Chief Secretary of the Cabinet, on July 18

(b)
Great Britain.
(1)
General Relations.
The recently increased tension in Anglo-Japanese relations was
reflected in debates in the British Parliament on July 26 and 27 in
which representatives of the British Government, responding to
Pp
solicitations that it take stronger action against Japan for the
protection

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.98)

proteotion of British interests in China, intimated that Great
Britain micht abrogate the Anglo-Japanese Comneroial Treaty of
19ll*. The situation was aggravated in Japan when it was reported
by the press that the British Arbassador had begun talks with
the Minister for Foreign Affairs with a view to assuring the pro-
tection of British interests in China**.
Earlier in the month the Japanese press was calm in its
editorial conments on British policy vis-à-vis China and Japan.
A favorable impression was created here by a report that the
British Governnent had refused to grant a loan of £ 20,000,000
to China. The vernacular newspapers expressed the opinion that
there had been a change of attitude by Great Britain on the side
of prudence in its dealines with Japan and China. However, this
favorable impression was altered upon the publication of reports
that Great Britain might extend non-military credits to China.
The above-mentioned debates in the British Parliament,
coupled with the talks reported by the press between Sir Robert
Craicie and General Ugaki concerning the protection of British
interests in China, were strongly dencunced in this country.
Although the Foreign Office stated that the anti-British views set
forth in the press did not in any way reflect the views of the
Foreign Office, the Foreign Office spokesnan stated at a press
conference on July 29 that the debates in the British Parliament
might be interpreted by the Japenese people as beins: in the nature
of a threat to Japan. According to the JAPAN TIMES of July 30,
the Minister for Foreign Affairs said at a five-Minister conference
on July 29 that sone of the recent interpellations in the British
Parliament might heve been made with the desire to bring pressure
on the British-Japanese negotiations in Tokyo which comnenced on

X. MISCELLANEOUS.
(1) Cancellation of the 1940 Tokyo Olympic Games
and Other Scheduled Events.
At a meeting held on July 15 the Cabinet decided to cancel
the Olympic Games scheduled to have been held in Tokyo in 1940.
Marquis Kido, the Welfare Minister, issued a statement on the sane
day to the effect that the Government had been forced to relinquish
the 1940 Olympic Games because of the "China Emergency", the
successful conclusion of which required the solid and united front
of the nation and the mobilizetion of all available man-power and
material assets. Durine the month it was also decided to cancel
the following scheduled events: the l940 International Exposition
in commemoration of the 2,600th anniversary of the founding of
the Empire; the World Recreation Conference to have been helc in
Osaka in 1940; the projected Italo-German-Japanese Amity Athletic
Meet scheduled for the Auturn of 1938; and six domestic expositions
that were to have been helc in 1938.
(2) Floods in Kobe.
fter the heavy floods thet cuased loss of life ane property
damage in the Tokyo cistrict et the end of June and during the
first few days of July, a more serious flood cccurred in Kobe, where
between 150 and 200 persons were estirated to have been killed and
over 2,ooo houses were saic to have been destroyed. According to
an announcement made by the Home Ministry on July 6, as a result
of the floods throughout Japan B60 persons wero killed, hurt, or
missing, and a total of nearly 400,000 houses were lost, wrecked,
or flooded.

(3) Proposed Elimination of Western Music from Japan.
Mr. Nagayo Motoori, a Directcr of the Dai Nippon Composers'
Association, was stated by the CHUGAI of July 25 to have proposed
a measure for adoption by the Government which would amost
completely eliminate Western music fron Japan. According to this
plan, Japan would seek to evolve a genuine music of its own; all
foreign compositions (whether classical or jazz) woulc be kept out
of the country; and all public concerts, operas, radio programs,
and phonograph records on Western lines would be banned. It is
said that Mr. Motoori's object is tc "heighten the Japanese spirit
through music".

On August 25, 1938 Rear Admiral Kiyosini Noda, spokesman
for the Navy Department, announced at his press conference ir
the morning with foreign correspondents that the attack by
Japanese naval planes on a "strange big plane" about thirty
miles from Hong Kong had been entirely justified. The plane,
he said, bore no distinguishable markings and acted in a "very
suspicious manner." On the approach of the Japanese, the
plane turned and hid in soe clouds apparently attempting to
escape. The Japanese, believing it to belong to the enemy,
pursued and justifiably attacked the plane wlich made a forced
Janding in a creck. It was only after this that e Chinese
character meaning "mail" was observed.

ceclared thet Japanese naval planes are strictly observing

urthinkable" that they should deliberately cttack passenger

Ambassador to Tokyo, Yuan Chen-tuo and the Italian Ambassador,
Mr. Auriti. The agrcenent is to become effective on September
lst, and to continue for one year. The agrcement provides for
perfectly balanced cxports and imports between Italy and
Japen-"Menchukuo" in value on the basis of the pound sterling.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL SITUATION.
(a) Political Parties.
During the month of August there were further reports in
the press concerning movements for & single political party
in Japan. On August 23, it was stated in the JAPAN TIMES,
giving its source as tho MIYAko ncwspaper, that Fusanosuke
Kuhara, formerly believod to have been implicated in the Febru-
ary 26, 1936, Incident, is determined to instigate a one-party
movement. He is supposedly supported by the Justice Minister
and the Minister for Education in the Goverument. It was also
rumored that the Minseito and the Seiyukai parties werc negotiat-
ing with the possible intent for emalgamation and that they were
hoping to form a new party under the leadership of General Ugaki,
the Forcign Minister. While these and other reports can only be
considered as rumors, they do indicate what is believed to be
a definite trend in Japanese politics away from multi-party
organization and toward c single netional party.
It was rumored in the press on August 26 that somc of the
political parties were advocating an extraordinary session of
the Diet to debate measures for handline the situation after the
fall of Hankow. While thcre mey be some discontent in party
circles it is not believed necessary to give serious consideration
to such rumors as this for an extraordinary Diet sessbn.
The general trend in Japanesc political affairs seems to be,
as it is in cconomic affairs, one of coordination and consolidation.
(b)
Central Organ for China Affairs.
There were many indications during the month under review
that the project for the formation of a politicnl organ to control
military, foreien, financial and industrial affairs in the occupied
creas

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
areas in China continued to bo the subjcct of serious negotiations
within the verious groups comprising the Government under Prime
Minister Konsyc. It has not as yet been possible to conciliate
the divergent opinions of the two lccding groups composod of the
Army and Navy on the one hand and the Foreign and Financicl
Ministries on the other. There were indications thet every effort
would be made to come to a decision on the "Chinn organ" by or
before the fall of Hankow.
(c) Mobilization
The Government continued its gradual process of mobilization
of the nation, and on August 25 promulgated an Imperial Ordinance
concerning the registration of physicians, dentists, pharmacists,
and nurses. It is cxpected that this registration of technical
capabilities of the population will be extended to all branches of
technical knowledge. While not thc first stop, it is onc of the
most important yet taken by the Government on the basis of the
powers granted by the National General Mobilization Law passed the
last session of the Dict. Prcctically all other mecsures taken
by the Governmont for the mobilization and control of materil
resources have been on thc basis of temporary lcgislation passed
at the last session of the Dict as an emereency measure and not
as e permanent feature such as is the National Mobilization Law.

General economic and financial conditions in Japan during
the month of August continuod to be in a somewhet indefinite
and uneasy condition. Tho nation's entire cconomic and financial
organizction is bcing subject to a process vaguely described as
national mobilization. This necessarily hinders long range
planning or commitment. The situction was, however, somewhat
cased by the official policy of oncoureging cxports and of giving
some definite aid to smell menufacturers and industrialists.
There were in addition other cids to the general situation such
as the improved economic conditions in the United States, the
trade agreement betwecn Japun-"Manchukuo" and Italy and the truce
agreement with the U.S.S.R.
Progress continued to be madc on plans for the cconomic
development of China with the cstablishment of a North China.
Telegraph and Telephone Company, Central China Elcctric Telegraph
Company, North China Cement Company and the Shanghai Inland
Navigation Company.
Considerable prominence was given in the prcss to the
spontaneous subscription by the public to tho shcres of the
North China Development Company and the Central Chinn Promotion
Company offered on August 12 as indicating the popularity of
Japan's ecenomic prorem for Chine.
These sheres were heavily
oversubscribed on the first day.
Further control mcasures of comioditics and reguletions

represent a considerable decree of trial and crror in the establish-
ing of a nationl mobiliz tion of thc cconomic structurc.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a) Airplane Flights in China:
China National Aviation Corporation Plane and
Pan American Airways.
11
It will be recalled that on August 26 the Ambassador made
representations to the Minister for Foreign ffairs against the
attack by Japanese naval planes upon the commercial airplane
belonging to the China National Aviation Corporation* and that
General Ugaki replied on August 3l by making a general disclaimer
of responsibility. In accordance with the Department's t elegraph-
ic instruction of September l4** Mr. Grew addressed a further note
to General Ugaki. The Ambassador's note pointed out that the at-
tacked plane was flying over a route which was wellknown and well
established; that the service was in accordance with a regularly
advertised schedule out of Hong Kong; that the plane carried
identification marks (Chinese characters) 5 x 4-1/2 feet in size
with the separate lines over five inches wide; that this informa-
tion could have been easily evailable to the Japanese authorities;
that it is the opinion of the Americen Government that the ex-
ercise of reasonable care might havo averted the incident in
question.
The Japanese Government, in its note of August 31, stated
that it held the view that the incident docs not involve the in-
terests of third Powers. In refutntion of this point the Ambassa-
dor's note reiterated that the life of merican nationals was
directly imperilled and that loss was suffored by Americen interests
vested in the China National Aviation Corporation. The note further
asserted that, obviously the incident was and is the material con-
cern of the American Government and people, and also that the Am-
erican
★
Embassy's Monthly Roport for August, peges 1,2,3,4.
*x
Department's telerrim No. 316, September 14, 1938, and
Embassy's despatcn No. 3265, September 22, 1938.

-2-
erican Government maintains the right to call to the ettention or
the Japanese Govornment any and all instancos of the imperilling
of American lives and rights in China by Japanese military forces.
It urgently requested that the Japaneso Government take steps form-
ally to implement its repeated assurances with regard to the rights
and interests of American nationals and more especially with regard
to the operation of Japanese military planes so as to remove the
possibility of attacks on the lives or proporty of non-combatant
civilians in such a way as to jeopardize the lives and interests
of American nationals.
The Ambassador's note was delivered by Mr. Dooman to Mr. Yoshizaw:
Director of the American Bureau of the Foreign Office, on September l
At the same time Mr. Dooman took occnsion to read to Mr. Yoshizawa
as under instruction from the Anerican Government part of the Depart
ment's telegram* stating in essence that the American Government
cannot but regard this attack as constituting one more of the mount-

1.
RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(a)
The Crisis in Europe: The President's Message.
The troulie in Europe which at one point was even successful
in driving the hostilities in China from the f ront page of the
Japanese press, was, of course, closely and carefully followed by
the Japanese Government and the more intelligent people. It pro-
vided some stimulus to the rabid nationalists who pressed for an
official declaration of sympathy for, and support of Germany. On
the whole the press was generally appreciative of the strenuous ef-
forts of Britain and France for a pacific solution, although it made
it perfectly clear that its sympathies lay entirely with Germany
and with German aspirations in Czechoslovakia. The appeal of the
President of the United States was prominently displayed, and for
the most part, sympathetically interpreted. The Ambassador's trans-
mission of the request of the Americen Government to Japan to assist
in t he urging of a continuence of negotiations on Germany was also wide
ly published. Although the general reaction in Japan seemed to be
sympathy with Germany in its aspirations, at the same time relief at
the avoidance of war wns expressed. The Foreign Office on September 14
issued a statement* to the effect that Japan "is prepared as ever to
join forces with Germany and Itely for fighting against Red opera-
tions in eccordance with the spirit of the enti-Comintern Agreement."
8

irging a continuance on Germany and Czechoslov: kia of efforts for a

(b) Great Britain.
During the month of September, relations with Great Britain
remained in a practical impasse. As the British Ambassador was
unable to obtain from the Foreign Minister in his conversations
with the latter any satisfaction concerning the five principal
British desiderata, and as the Japanese attitude which had stiffen-
ed during August remaired coldly formal, little or no progress was
made.
During the course of the month under review, the British Em-
bassy made formal representations et the Foreign Office concerning
the following matters: Whangpoo Conservancy Board; Mengchiang oil
monopoly*; Chinese Customs Administration**; Chinese salt adminis-
tration; the right of navigation of the Yangtze by third party
nationnls; the safety zone at Hankow; return of British nationals
to their property in Nanking***; occupation of British property
by Japanese forces in China****; British cleims for indemnity in
the Yangtze River incidents of December 5, 11 and 12, 1937*****.

In southwest Shansi the
Japanese forces continued fighting close to the Yellow River but
did not effect a crossing. At Yuanku Chinese forces pushed across
the Yellow River and took refuge in the mountains to the north.

In a similar manner the push from Luchow seemed to have "bogged

in front of Hankow, the objective, are for the time being proving to
be an effective barrier. This particular movement has been divided
into two groups, one, going westward at right angles to the direct
line to Hankow, succeeded on September 16 in taking Kwangchow. The
second group, which is following a more direct line towards Hankow,
turned into the mountains to the southwest, making Maching its ob-
jective.
The Japenese forces on the north bank of the Yangtze were
able by September 29 to capture the river fort at Tienkiachen,
a point roughly 70 miles by air line from Hankow. They met with
stiff resistence on the part of the Chinese forces. The Japanese
moving up the river on the south bank were successful in occupying
positions epproximately corresponding to those on the north bank.
The Japanese forces south of the Yangtze River, which had
turned southward toward Teien also were experiencing vigorous re-
sistance and wore making only slight progress at the expense of a
considerable number of casualties. The appearance of a group of
Japanese forces to the west of Teian indicated a movement looking
to the surrounding und cutting off of Teian. While this flanking
movement to the south has little direct importance in the actual
capture of Hankow, it is effective in maintaining large numbers
of Chinese forces in that area and preventing their aiding in a
direct defense of Hankow and at the sume time serves as an effective

It was announced on September 20 that the Council of the

in Japan was calm and the press prophesied immediate rejection of
the League's invitation. The invitation was later officially re-
jected by the Foreign Office. It was suggested in Japan that the
various countries of the Leegue would take little or no action lead-

(g) Peru.
On September 14 there arrived in Japen from Peru a group des-
cribed as thc "Peruvian Economic ana Cultural Mission to Japan.*
It is only natural that the visit at this time of such prominent
and substantial Peruvians as compose this mission has given rise
to considerable speculation as to the real or potential purpose of
the Mission. The economic connections betweenthe two countries both
in trade and in vested interests do not represent an item warranting
the expenditure of the time and money necessary for such a venture
either on the part of the Japanese or the Peruvians. The explanation
has been advanced that Japan is attempting to draw Peru within the
orbit of the anti-Comintern group. It is no doubt true that Japan
seeks to obtain as many international friends as possible. However,
the local press has emphasized the cultural and economic aspects of
the Mission and it has been authoritatively denied that there is any
political objective connected with the visit of the Peruvians. The
Embassy was informed that the initiative for the sending of t he
Peruvian Mission to Japan came from quasi-official organizations,
the Society for International Cultural Relations, the Japan Chamber
of Commerce, and the Association of Exporters to Central and South
America. The Embassy has also learned that the Japanese Government
hes no connection with the Mission other than heving extended the
invitation to the Peruvian Government on behalf of these Japanese
organizations. If, however, the visit should pave the way toward
official negotiations for a new treaty of commerce with Peru it
willhave achieved  very desirable result from the standpoint of
ne Japanese.

III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL SITU.TICN.
(a) The Cabinet.
294
The debate on the formulation of plans for the establishment
of a political institution or central organ for the development,
control, and execution of all affairs involving Japan and C hina has
been the most upsetting influence in the Cabinet since its forma-
tion under Premier Konoe. To the difference of opinions over this
major question has been attributed the resignation of former Foreign
Minister Koki Hirota and the reorganization cf the Cabinet in the
spring of this year. At least a dozen different times during the
month of September there were headlines in the press concerning
compromises, revisions, and deadlocks in the controversy. This
was climaxed suddenly and cntirely unexpectedly by the resignation
of Foreign Minister Ugaki on the t wenty-ninth of Septcmber.
The
* Embassy's telegram No. 611, September 20, 1938.

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.98)
20
The original plen, according to the ASAHI, was for a boarā to
take over the control of the administration of China affairs gen-
erally, economic, financial, cultural, diplomatic, etc., and tue
supervision f the North China Devalopment Compary and the Cortral
China Promotion Company in eddition to handling all matters relating
to Japan's policy towards China.
Although completely authoritative information is unavailable
it is believed that the Foreign Minister's objections, in addition
to being based on legal or constitutional grounds, was that a board
or political institute of such scope was unnecessary at the present
time end particularly was unnecessary es a permanent addition to
Jepan's political structurc. It is obvious also that such an in-
stitution would preempt many of the prerogatives of the Foreign
Office in the conduct of relntions between Japan and Chinn and
between Japen and third Powers with interests in China. Immediate-
ly prior to the Foreign Minister's resignation, however, it was
stated in the press that subordinates of the various Ministries
had been successful in reaching a compromise which was to be ap-
proved the following day.
The vernacular press was practically unanimous in its expres-
sion of regret nnd disappointment for General Ugaki's resignation and
there was considerable censure of his action taken at e time when
unity in political affnirs is most urgently desired. The TokYo
NICHI NICHI went so far as to point to the inspiring example of the
statemanship and spirit cf conciliation and cooperation evidenced
by the European Powers in their recent crisis, as an example, em-
phasizing the contrast showing a lack of conciliation and coopera-
tion in the Konoe Cabinet as evidenced by the resignation of Foreign
Minister Kazushige Ugaki.

(b) Statements by Government Officials.
In a talk bbefore Members of the House of Peers, on September ?
Mr. Seihin Tkeda, Finance and Commerce-Industry Minister, stated
that the nation must be fully prepared to meet the continuance
of economic control measures even long after the fall of Hankow.
This is in complete accord with his statement to the press on Septem-
ber 17 at his villa in Oiso. At that time Mr. Ikeda stated that
the long-term reconstruction of China would require continuance
over a long period of the economic measures taken in Japan to meet
the present emergency.
(c) Changes in the Diplomatic Service.*
In the early part of September the ASAHI predicteā that Mr.
Yoshida, Ambassador to Great Britain, would retire to be succeeded
by Mr. Shigemitsu, Ambassador to Russia; Mr. Togo, Ambassador to
Germany, to go to Moscow, and Lieutenant-General Oshima, to become
Ambassador to Germany. On September 8 it was announced that these
changes have been definitely decided upon, and in addition that
Mr. Toshiro Shiratori, former Minister to Stockholm, was desig-
nated Ambassador to Rome. At the same time there were some rumors
to the effect that Ambassador Saito would return from the United
States and be replaced by the present Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Mr. Horinouchi.

Preliminary foreign trade figures for the month of September
indicated that exports hed been approximately Yen 241,958,000 and
imports Yen 205,114,000. The total for the first nine months of
this yeer would therefore total approximately Yen 1,894,571,000
with imports totalling Yen 2,Q01,606,000. This represents a decline
of some 17 per cent in exports and 22 per cent in imports as com-
pared with last year. These figures are not to be taken at their
face value for the reason that in such a brief report as this it
is

impossible to teke into consideration various factors, such as
lower commodity prices compared with those of last year. In edditicr
the foreign trade with countries which could supply Japan with
foreign exchange has witnessed a much greater decrease than is shown
by the above percentages. The fact that exports to "yen bloc". 
(Manchukuo" nd other occupied parts of China) areas have been very
heavy has tended to prevent the percentage decline of the total for-
eign trade of Jepan from being as great as it would have been other-
wise. It has been estimated that exports to foreign exchange coun-
tries has declined over 40 per c ent. This means that a further
burden is thrown upon Japan's foreign exchange and the netion's
ability to purchase and sell in foreign exchange (dollar or sterling)
markets).
(b) Generel conditions.
The unsettled conditions ir. Europe were a definite factor tend-
ing to ircrease the already existing lethargy in business activity,
excepting, of course, war industries.
Business and financial circles
in Japan fully realized the economic losses which would accrue to
Japan in the event of a widesprend conflict in Europe. There were,
of course, other depressing factors: the official announcoment
that rigid control of Japan's economy will continue long efter the
fall of Hankow, the certainty of an oven larger nationel budget for
1939-40 which is almost certain to bring higher taxes, the continued
depressed condition of exports and, finally, extensive and heavy
losses resulting from natural disasters such as typhoons and floods.
Somewhat offsetting these depressing tendencies were the facts
nat the volume of exports continued to exceed that of imports,
he German-"Manchukuo" and the Japan-"Manchukuo"- Italy trade pacts
re inaugurated, and the news of the consolidation of the provisional
governments

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
-24-
vernments in the occupied areas in China which would tend to in-
dicate a more stable political situation in those areas.
Further economic control measures were instituted during the
month of Sep- wber such as regulations for the control of distribu-
tion of coal, price regulations of copper, import limitations for
potassium sulphate, standardized prices for woollen yarn, foreign
style paper, ice, rubber goods, enamelled ware, scrap iron, etc.
(c) Financial Trends.
The stock market dur ing the month of September was in a de-
pressed and confused condition owing to an overabundance of un-
attractive new share issues coupled with the tense European situation:
resulting in a large scale flight of gold from Europe to the United
States and higher London gold bar prices. In view of this general
situation operators in the market were generally reluctant to make
any future commitments. The money market has recently experienced
unusually low rates. Yen-dollar rates declined considerably owing
entirely to the unusual decline in the New York-London cross rate.
Yen-sterling rates have been maintained at the established rate of
1 shilling 2d.
(d) Shipping.
79475
It was announced in the press on September 10 that the Com-
munications Office had decided to include in its budget for the
next fiscal year an appropriation to subsidize the construction
of small and medium sized merchant vessels. The total amount of
the subsidy was stated to be Yen 6,0oo,0oo to be granted at the rate
of Yen 30 per ton for ships of tess than 4,300 tons.
