Reflecting a strong demand for shipping between Japan and
China, the N.Y.K. line announced its decision to place two new
liners on the Kobe-Shanghai route beginning next month. The TAIYo
MARU is to be taken from the Pecif.c service and the BOKU YO MaRU
to be removed from the South American service and both vossels are
to be assigned to the Shanghai run.
Mr. Shozo Murata, President of the Osaka Shosen Kaisha,on his
return from China on September 2, announced that the Nisshin Steam-
ship Company would soon resume full activity in shipping on the
Yangtze. He stated, acccrding to the NICHI NICHI, that "at present,
however, the company is engaged in cargo transportation on the
Yangtze, chartering.three:ships from the O.S.K. and one from the
N.Y.K." He also said that the Japanese company was perfecting
definite plans for effective competition with British shipping on
the Yangtze when it is reopened to foreign boats.
It was reported on September 30 that final decision had been
reached in the organization of a company to consolidate shipping
interests between Japan, North Korea, and "Manchukuo".
The company
is to be capitalized at Yen 40,000,J00.

X. MISCELLANEOUS.
994.48
On September 1st a record typhoon struck the Kwanto area
causing extensive damage. Incomplete reports were to the offect
that at least 50 persons had been killed and thousands of homes
flooded or destroyed. Considerable damage was done to shipping in
Yokohama and other harbors. Communicetion facilities were severe-
ly disrupted. On September 5 a strong gale struck the island of
Shi :oku causing considerable damage. These and other storms and
disasters have marked this year as bing particularly notoworthy for
the total emount of damage resulting from natural disnsters.
On Septomber 9, the report of the typhoon losses was formally
presented to the Emperor. In summary, it stated that more than
1,Ooo,0oo acres of tilled land were affected, including approximately
330,000 acres of paddy fields and about 10,000 acres of fruit orchards.
There was no mention in the newspaper item of the pucuniary damage
which resulted.
September 12 to Septomber 16 was devoted to the "most extonsive
onti-air raid moneuvels yet held in the Kwanto cren".

Tokyo's Monthly Report for November,
1938.
The first eight pages deal with Japan's
reply of November 19 to the American note of
October 6 and with Mr. Dooman's conversa-
tion and Mr. Grew's conversation with the
Foreign Minister in the latter part of
November, all of which have been dealt with
by the Embassy in telegrams and despatches
previously received.
Pages 9 to 12 review Mr. Grew's
conversations in regard to navigation on
the Yangtze, already reported.
The Japanese Foreign Minister notified
Mr. Grew in a note on November 7 of the
dangers to American interests in western
areas of China after the fall of Canton and
Hankow. This was similar to previous
warnings after the fall of other important
centers in China and Mr. Grew made no reply
as the views of the American Government in
this regard had been repeatedly made clear
already. Evidently the Embassy did not
report the above-mentioned note in a
separate despatch. (Page 13).
The

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
The American Embassy's representations
with regard to the Customs at Canton are
reported on page l2, with regard to embargo
on sheep and slink wool and hemp in north
China on page 14, with regard to Americans
at Kuling on page 15, with regard to various
violations of American rights and interests
on page 16.
Pages 17 to 28 deal with Japan's
relations with countries other than the
United States. All material contained
therein has already been received in other
forms with the possible exception of a
statement of Baron Mitsui on page 28, who,
in reference to the possibility of Japan
and Hungary concluding a cultural pact,
stated that Japan should have countries in
Central Europe surrounding Soviet Russia
understand the true cultural aspect of Japan
and of the Japanese spirit.
General business conditions showed
little, if any, improvement during November
(page 34). The depressed condition was
contributed to by the Finance Minister's
statement that economic control is to be
continued indefinitely, by the decision of
the Government to invoke Article 1l of the
National General Mobilization Law, and by
the somewhat disturbed and difficult
condition of Japan's relations with third
countries.

and a Mitsubishi associate and a representa-

business in China in a major way, is given
on page 36. Such a development would be
either (1) through Japanese interests making
way for themselves by enoroaching upon the
trade of established companies with resulting
uneconomic consequenoes or (2) through the
creation of harmonious and close relations
between the two interested groups. The
suggestion was put forward of a joint
enterprise, embracing Japanese, American,
British, and Chinese capital.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
1.
Japanese Reply to the Ambassador:s Note of
October 6, i938, concerning American Kights
and Interests in China.
On November 16 the Ambassador was told infornally by the
Minister for Foreign Affairs that the Japanese reply to our
ncte of October 6 concerning American interests in China would
probably be delivered to the Embassy on November 19. On the
same day Foreign Minister Arita told the Counselor, Mr. Dooman:
that he would like to talk with him on Saturday, November 19,
9
at two o'clock, not on a basis of official formality but es
"between two old friends". The Ambassador approved, as it was
expected that Mr. Dooman's interview would result in interest-
ing disclosures concerning future Japanese policy and would be
a very helpful prelude to the Ambassador's conversation with
the Foreign Minister scheduled for the following Monday.*
On the evening of November 18** the Foreign Office handed
to the Embassy the note of the Japanese Government replying to
the Ambassador's note of Cctober 6. The English translation
furnished by the Foreign Office was immediately telegraphed to
the Department. While the major part of the Japanese note was
devoted to an explanation of certain particular Japanese inspirea
activities in China, the most outstanding fact presented was
that the Japanese Government takes the stand that there exists
in the Fer Enst today conditions to which former concepts and
prirciples are no longer applicable and that it is the firm
conviction of the Jepanuse Government that any attempt to apply
to
* mbassy': telegrom Nc. 732, November 16, 1938.
**Embnssy's telegram No. 7:3. November 18, 1058.

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to these present or to future conditions such concepts and
principles will not in the least contribute to the solution
of the immediate problems or promote the firm establishment
of enduring peace in Enst Asia.
The Japanese Government reiterated its determination to
givo full respect to American rights and interests in China
and declared that it is its intentinn to restore normal condi-
tions as soon as circumstances permit. As rogards future
American participation in the economic activities in China,
it is stated thet the Japanese Government has no intention
of objecting to the participation of Americans or of nationals
of other third countries in all fields of trade and industry
providing, however, that such participation is undertaken
with the complete understanding of the meaning of the Japanese
Governm-nt's pronouncement on policy.
The specific instances of discrimination which were set
forth in the Ambassador's note of October 6 re not d ealt with
in the Japanese reply.
The question of the promotion companies
which have been organized recently by the Japanese Government
is dealt with by the statement that it is only through these
companies that Japan can provide China with the urgently
necessary assistance for its rehabilitation and development.
Surprise was expressed in the Japaneso reply at the
allegation of disparity between the treatment accorded Japen-
ose in Americn and the treatment nccorded Americans in Japan
and it was asserted that economic restrictions to which
Anericans may: be subject at the present time are imposed not
upon Americans alone but equally upon all forigners as
ren Japanese. The Japanese Government reserved for another
time the presentation of Japan's views on the treatment of
pnnese subjects in American territory.
Generally

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
-3..
Generally speaking the reply cannot be considerec 
satisfactory response to the Anerican desiderata presentel
in the Ambassador's originel note to the Japanese Government.
However, Foreign Minister Arita undertook in his interview
with Mr. Dooman to set forth in more detail the Japanese
position*. In answering Mr. Arita's question concerning
the Ambassador's reaction to the Japanese note whicn was
handed to Mr. Dooman on the evening before, the Counsclor
replied that although the Ambassador had exemined the note
with the best of good will he could not find in it any state-
ment substantially responsive to the desires of the American
Government. Mr. Arita then proceeded to set forth the positicn
of the Japanese Government. He stated that Japan, being then 
weak country, had no alternative but to support the United
States in its efforts to implement the application of the prin-
ciple of the Open Door to Chine toward the end of the last
century. Mr. Arita umphasized, however, thet the principle of
the Open Door was at first applied to matters of relatively
small importance, but that after t he Nashington Treaty was
concluded the motive
of most of the Powers in support-
ing that principle was to enable them to exploit China largely
as they had been exploiting ifrice. The position of Japan, the
Foreign Minister pointed out, wes entirely difforent from that
of the United States and the British Empire which are, he said,
large territoricl, political, ond economic entities. Jopan,
he stated, was extremely vulnerable to a program of economic
sanctiors.
* Embassy's despatch No. 3455, November 25, 1938.

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)

sanctions. Mr. irita emphasized that while the term "bloc"
had been applied to Japan's economic program for the Japan-
"Manchukuo"-China areas, this term was very apt to give rise
to considerable misunderstanding nnd that it was not Japan's
intention to create an economic bloc at all analogous to
that of the British Empire. Japan, he said, has no intention
whatever of assimilating politically any part of Chinn or of
"Manchukuo". The proposed arrangement would not necessarily
be exclusive of American and other foreign enterprise and capital.
it is merely to provide Japan with a market and a source for
raw materials offering to other countries at the same time an
opportunity for trade and investment.
Mr. Arita further stated that in spite of the prevalence
of the widespreid feeling that Japan has now adopted a new
policy - one of closing the Open Door in China - there has in
fact been no change in policy. He referred to the assurances
given on several occasions by his several predecessors, to the
American, British and other representatives in Tokyo, tlat Japan
would respect the principle of the Open Door and added that as
a matter of fact those assuronces were not intended to be un-
conditionnl since Japan could no longer give an unqualified
undertaking to respect the Open Door. He asserted that the
assurances of his predecessors had been given in good faith in
that they were attompting to reconcile the principle of the
Open Door with Japan's actual needs and objectives, but that
this could not be done. He added that, as he had previously
explained, those objectives ere to provide Japan with a secure
market and a safe source of necessary raw materials against
any possible threat of economic senctions and that within
those

those limits Japan was prepared to guarantee equality of

significantly added that he hed not repeated these assurences
V
in the note which was sent the day before because he had de-

therefore deliberately avoided the opening up of e dispute

(4) Notification by Japanese of the Expansion of the
Area of Hostilities.
Under date of November 7 the Minister for Foreign Affairs
formally notified the Ambassador that following the fall of
Canton and.Hankow the area of hostilities would henceforth
gradually expand toward the west, that Shensi Province, Hupeh
Province, and Hunan Province were in the near future to be-
come areas of hostilities, and that even west of these areas
up to a line connecting Suchow, Batang, and Tali, military ob-
jectives within Chinese territory were to become objects of
attack by Japanese military airplanes. In this connection
the Minister for Foreign Affairs presented his Government's
urgent request that the flight over these areas of aircraft
having any connection with the United States be prohibited,
travel in the above-mentioned areas be undertaken at the risk
of the travelers, wherever possible American citizens with-
drawing to safe areas, notification to the Japanese authorities
concerning American property interests be expedited and such
property be clearly marked. The Foreign Minister's note stated
that it was impossible fully to protect interests which are
not so marked, that property transferred from Chinese to
American ownership in order to avoid confiscation by the Jap-

II. RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
(1) China.
(a) Military Operations.
With the accomplishment of the two immediate major ob-
jectives, the capture of Hankow and Canton, during the month
of October, Japanese military operations in China for the month
of November were largely confined to what are considered to be
preparatory operations, presumably for a further advance. At-
19
tempts were made to consolidate recently occupied areas and
strenuous efforts were exerted to "mop up" the guerrilla rem-
nants within the occupied areas. The entire month represented
a distinct lull in active military operations.
After the capture of Hankow, certain units of Japanese
forces turned southward on the Hankow-Canton Railway and suc-
ceeded in occupying Yochow on the llth. The further objective
was reported to be Nanchang. South of Teian, Japanese and
Chinese forces continued to face one another along the Hsiushui
River but hostilities were not particularly active. North of
Hankow the Japanese forces carried out operations along the
slope of the Tapieh Mountains and west of the railway in order
to dispose of remnants of the Chinese forces which had been
surprised

surprised and caught by the rapid Japanese movement up the
Yangtze River.
In South China military operations were largely devoted
to the ccasolidation of the hastily occupied areas around
Canton and the "cleaning up" of remaining forces in the deltc
areas south of Canton.
In spite of many reports which indicate that a determined
Japanese offensive was to be undertaken in Shensi the fighting
in that area was of a desultory nature. During the month of
November a considerable number of canpaigns against guerrillas
operating in the occupied arces was reported.
On Novembor ll General Moto-o Furusho, reportedly in ill
health, was relieved as Supreme Commander of the South China
forces by Lieutenant General Rikichi Ando.
(b) General.
With the possitle exception of the military operations
there were, of course, no relations between Japan nd the
recognized Government of Chine. However, Japan continued to
announce plans for and to undertake various activities looking
to the ccnsolidation and development of Japan's new position
in the occupied aroas. It was announced in the latter part of

for China was to take place on November 7.*
The inaugural meet-

Japan's plans with regard to economic reconstruction and ex-

The activities
of these companies will undoubtedly, in the first instance, be
directed toward harbor, communication, transportation and public
utility facilities. At the sane time whcrever possible, prepara.

There was considerable teik concerning the forntion of
the projected "economic bloc" comprising Japan, "Manchukuo"
and China. An economic conference was held in Tokyo concern-

scheduled to be held shortly thereafter in Osaka end Moji.

resign that position end to become the highest adviser to a new
Central Government which is to be established in China. Nego-

the month of November betwen the Nanking and Peiping temporery
Governments but no successful conclusions were forthcoming.
Various articles and newspaper stories appearing from time
to time seem to indicate that Japan has not yet been able to
formulete detailed and concrete plans for the exploitation of
China and the formation of the new "economic bloc".
It appears
that even the Japanese themselves are not yet aware of the
extent to which they can establish a new economic system in
East Asia nd that the details of their plans have not yet been
settled.
(2) Great Britain.
Prior to the opening of the Parliament in London it was
to be expucted that the British Government through its Embassy
in Tokyo would continue to press the Japanese Government for

definite evidences of an implementation of the assurances

in China.* In view of previous assurances given the British
Embassy that the situation es rogards navigation on the Yangtze
would probably be eased by the fall of Hankow, it was also to

Powers in that country". In this interview Mr. Arite: present-

interview which has been covered in a prior section of this repor
Mr. Arita's presentation of the Japanese views at least indi-
cated a desire on the part of the Japanese Government to reach
some practical solution of the difficulties with the Powers.**
The frank declaration of Japanese policy towards China
brought Anglo-Japaneso relations to a point where Great Britain
was faced with the necessity of either pursuing a course which
would result in some practical working arrangement which would
harmonize present British endeavors in China with the announced
and determined policy of Japan to dominate and to exploit China
for strategic reasons or of giving assistance to China in ade-
quate measure to enable the resistance of Chieng Kai-shek to be
successful, or finally of following a policy in which protests
are made for each violation of rights and interests and deter-
mination not to recognize Japanese-made changes is maintained.
While Mr. Arita told the British Ambassador, as he also told
Mr. Dooman, that the previous assurances given by his prede-

siderations of the Japanese were to be fostered and encouraged.
There still circulated in Tokyo rumors to the effect that

marized in five points which indicated that the Domei writer
believed that Great Britain has realized the collapse of the

ambitions in China, and is willing to cooperate with Japan
providing that country devotes itself to the maintenance of
peace and ordor in China.* The British Embassy, however,
categoricnlly denied that there was any truth in such state-
ments.**

and Manchuria, was lavishly entertained in the usual Japanese

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Statements by Government Officials.
On November 2 the press section of the Foreign Office
made available to the press and to fcreign correspondents a
document entitled "Statement of the Japanese Government,
November 3, 1938".** On November 3 Premier Konoe read this
statement in a radio broadcast and made further explanations of
the Government's attitude. The outstanding points of this
officiel statement*** are that Japan is striving for the es-
tablishment of a new order in East Asia to assure permanent
stability and peace in that aren. This new order is to be
founded upon the tri-partite cooperation and coordination of
Japan, "Manchukuo" and Chine in economic, political, culturel
and
* Embessy's despatch No. 3417, November 4,1938.
**Department's telegram No. 375, November 3, 5 p.m., and Embassy's
telegram No. 703, November 4, 10 a.m.
**Embassy's despatch No. 3416, November 4, 1938.

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.98)
-30-
and other fields. Japan is desirous of having China take an
active part in the creation of this new order and will not
reject participation even by the Kucmintang Government if it
repudirtes some of its present personnel. If: however, the
Kuomintang Government, which, the statoment says, has become
a mere local régime, persists in its communistic and anti-
Japanese policy, Jepan will never relent in its effort to
crush that régime. The Premier in his speech stated that
Japan seeks the cooperation of awakened Chinese in the es-
tablishment of China as an independent state and that Japan,
"Manchukuo" and Chine, each having its own individuality, ere
naturally and historioally bound together in a common mission
having as its purpose the peace and order of the Far East.
Premier Konoe bernted the National Government of China for
its betrayal of Chinese tradition and culture and for having
foiled tc aivance its common mission with Jaran. He reiterated
that Japan will not reject participation by the National Govern-
ment in this mission if that Government renovates and revises its
policies and personnel.
Regarding relations with third countries, the Premier's
radio address said that peace and independonce in China have
often been menaced by the imperialistic struggles of Occidental
Fowers and added that Japan is determined to establish true
international justice in all phases of human activity, including
commerce, immigration, resources end cul ture, and that Japan
will neither exclude cooperation of otiers providing such
cooperation is based upon an understanding of the new situa-
tion in East Asia, nor damage the legitimate rights of third
Powers in China.

(b) Political Parties.*
On November 15 the national movement sponsored by the
Minseito and Seiyukai, the major political parties, to assist
in the "spiritual mobilization" of the nation and to facilitate
cooperation with the Government's policies got under way at a mass
meeting held at the Hibiya Public Hall in Tokyo. This first
meeting was highly successful. On November 17, it was announced
that Premier Konoye was to discuss with political leaders of
the parties his plan for the "reorganization of the nation".
At the scme time the ASAHI reporteu thnt Premier Konoye con-
sidered that this new movement must be of a broader scope than
merely that of a national party. Count Arima, Minister for
Agriculture and Forestry, and Mr. Akira Kazami, Chief Secretary
of the Cabinet, were reportedly requested by the Premier to
prepare a draft plan of organization.** Subsequent develop-

VI. GENERAL ECONOMIC ANT FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.*
While it might have been expected that the fall of Canton
and Hankow and the strengthening of the Cebinet by the appoint-
ment of two new Ministers one for Foreign Affairs ard one for
Overseas Affairs wculd have contributed to improve business
sentiment, general business conditions during the month of
November showed little, if any, improvement. No doubt the
Finance Minister's statenent that economic control is to be
continued indefinitely contributed to this somewhnt depressed
condition. During the month of November, the decision of the
Government authorities to invoke Article ll of the National
General Mobilization Law along with tho other brond measures
provided in that law, contributed to widespread apprehonsion in
business and financial circles and precipitated one of the most
serious stock market brenks which hes occurred since the
hostilities in China bugen. The somewh't disturbed and diffi-
cult condition of Japan's relations with third countries also
ndded to the uneasy situation in economic circles.
There were several additional important control measures
promulgated or put into effect during the month of November.
On November 1st a ministerial order modifying regulations govern-
ing the sale of North American softwoods become effective.
These regulations are aimed at control of the consumption or
such lumber and are not expected to affect significantly imports
of
* Prepared with the assistance of the Commercial Attaché's Office.

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
imports of American lumber. It was announced that beginning
January 1 the Government will institute a quota system for the
distribution of fertilizers, a highly important factor in
Jepanese gricultural economy. During November the Ministry
of Commerce and Industry announced its decision to establish
a Scrap Iron Distribution Control Council made up of Govern-
ment officinls and representative of private interests. The
purpose of this Council is similar to the purpose of other
councils which have previously been formed for other commodities,
that is, to control prices, supply and distribution of the
material in question. On November 25 the Department of Commerce
issued revised retulations concerning the control of the sale
of gasoline and heevy oils. Under this revision refiners and
sellers of these products are rastricted -- they had hitherto
not been affected by the control measures in force. There
were also issued further regulations and revisions of regulations
concerning hides and leather, rayon textiles and yarn, etc.
Money rutes during the month of November were on the whole
easy and the absorption of Government bonds was reportedly
satisfactory. Owing to the downward tendency of the London-

per ￥ 100.

VII. ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMERCIAL INTERESTS.
During the menth of November the representative of & large
American oil company in Japan had an interview with Mr. Tani,
the Japanese "Ambassador-at-largo" to China and a Mr. Nomura
who has been associnted with the Mitsubishi interests in
importing oil into Japan. The significent fact brought out
in this interview was that it was the opinion of these two
Japanese that Japan intended to perticipate in the oil business
in China in a major way. The suggestion was mado that there
were two ways in which the cil business could operate under
the new conditions: "(1) Through Japanese interests making
way for themselves by encrcaching upon the trade of establish-
ed companies with resulting uneconcmic consequences, or (2)
thrcugh the creation f harmcnious and close relations between
the two intcrestcd groups." This suggestion was elaborated
upon and extended by the further suggestion that the present
fcreign companies having interests in the cil business in Chine
participate in a jcint enturprise plan embracing Japanese,
American, British, and Chincse capital. This new company
wculd then take cver plant and ther cepital investments of the
established companies in China and wculd rect refineries and
expand distributicn facilities along with the progress of the
anticipated development of Chine. In reply to the somewhat urgent
suggestions by Mr. Nonura the American company's representative
esserted that the China business of his company is under separate
jurisdiction

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.97)
-3
jurisdiction and that his view on the subject would be of no
value, but that the scheme suggested by the Japanese is so
contrary to the practices of th= c ompeny that it obviously
would require very careful cxana ion before any worthwhile
comment coula be made.*

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
(a)
Ambassador's Interviews with the Foreign Minister.
At the request of the Minister for Foreign Affairs,
Mr. Grew called on him on Decemter 8*. The Minister, believing
that it would be helpful to have freguent informal conversations
with the Ambassador, stated that he had requested this interview
in order to reply to some of the representations which the
Ambassador had made to him in the course of their conversation
on November 2l**. The Minister also said that in view of the
somewhat complicated nature of the subject under reference he
had written down his oral comments which he would read to the
Ambassador. After reading this statement to the Ambassador,
the Foreign Minister stated that he was to see the British
Ambassador that afternoon and thereupon read to Mr. Grew the
statement which he intended to make to Sir Robert Craigie. In
this interview the Ambasuador found a stiffer attitude in
phraseology on the pert of the Minister than in their last
conversation, The Minister suggested that little improvement
could be expected until Chiang Kai-sek had entirely been
eliminated. He also spoke with greet bitterness of the alleged
support by Greet Britain of the Generalissimo and intimated that
official publicity in England had. b: angering the military
people here. rendered the British situation in East Asia much
more difficult.
In Mr. Arit   rut statement he reiterated the position
taken by the Japanese evernment that while existing treaties
may have been calculatea to prevent international friction in

The entire statement was filled with ambiguous and indefinite

(c) Soviet Russia.
The fall of each year since the negotiating of the new
741.94
Fisheries Agreement between Soviet Russia and Janan in 1936
has brought efforts on the part of the Japanese to induce the
Soviets to sign this or a similar agreement. Each time the
Soviets have delayed until the last moment and have only signed
provisional agreements extending the existing situation for one
year.
On September 2lst tho Japanese Ambassador in Moscew was
instructed to open the negotintions. The Russians delcyed and
alleged that cortain conditions, such as Japanese viclations of
the Portsmouth Treaty, stationing of troops in Manchur a, the
closing of the Suya Straits, end also the non-paynent by
"Manchukuo" of th: final instalment in settlenent of tI transfer
of the North Manchiria Railway, needed to bo adjusted before
a new fisheries agrcemont would be signed. Nothing was accomplish-
ed on either side and Anbassador Togo replaced Shigemitsu,
bringing with him instructions to press the Russians on the
settlement of the Fisherics question. Considerable prominence
was given in the press in Japan to the "truculent ettitude" of
the Russians, and a delogation of Japanese interested in these
fisheries

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.98)
-16-
fisheries came to Tokyo to press the Government for decisive
action. Mr. Litvinov, however, up to the end of December,
the date on which the present agreement expired, maintained
his stanc and refused to meet the demands of the Japanese until
they in turn were to give satisfaction to the Russian desiderata.
It was quite definitely stated in Tokyo that the Japanese
intended to fish in Russian waters whether or not Russia entered
into a new agreenent covering thesc enterprises. The Japanese,
however, added that they were ready at any tine to resume
negotiations on this difficult question.
Fishing in Siberian weters usually begins in the early
part of April and while there apparently remains considerable
time for negotiations prior to the fishing season, the fact
that Russia has allowed the Treaty to expire indicates that
Russo-Japanese relations are in a morc involved state than they
have been since 1936. This, coupled with thc strong stand taken
by the Japanese, represents a situation which may at any time
become extremely scrious and in spite of the fact that these
difficulties in Sovict-Japenese relations cre perrennial in
their appearance, a situation exists which merits close obeerva-
tion.

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.98)

III: INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
(a) Important Statements by Government Officials.
On December 19 the Minister for Foreign Affairs in his
interview with the foreign press representatives issued a
prepared statement. This statoment sets forth largely the
same position as that expressed by the Minister to Mr, Dooman
in their interview on November 19th.* On December 22 Premier
Konoye issued a statement of basic importance. This set forth
the "moderate and magnanimous" as they were termed by the
Japanese press - Japanese terms for peace with China. These
terms may be summarized as follows:
(1) China's recognition of "Manchukuo";
(2) conclusion of a Sino-Japanese agreement to combat
Communism, this agreement to grant Japan the right to station
troops in specified areas in China and to designate Inner
Mongolia as a "specificd anti-Communist Area";
(3) Economic cooperation between Japan and China, includ-
ing the "freedom of residence and trade on the part of Japanese
subjects to the interior of China" and "China to extend to Japan
facilities" for the development of natural resources, particu-
larly in North China and Inner Mongolia.
This statement was phrased, as have been all recent
official statements, in very general terms and was filled with
qualifying clauses. This was probably done with the deliberate
intent to permit Japen all possible latitude to pursue whatever
course circunstances may warrent. The peace terms in thenselves
are not new. However, the gurantees which Chine may be required
to give in order to assure that country's observance of the
terms will undoubtedly be far more important.
It is quite probable thet this statement in view of the
moderate languagc used and the tone of the press comment was
intended to invite the Unitcd States and Eneland to consider
the ultimate Japanese intentions in China**

VI.
GENERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS
(a)
Foreign Trade.
The foreign trade of the Japanese Empire during 1938
was considerebly lower in volume and in value than that of the
previous year. The Finence Ministry reported that there was
an excess of exports amounting to Y69,498,000. This favorable
trade balance, however, represented trade with all countries
including the "yen bloc" areas. It was stated that trade with
third countries resulted in an excess of imports amounting to
m,ooo,ooo. No figures are available to indicate how far this
unfavorable balance is offset by Japan's invisible exports during
the year. As a considerable pert of Japanese merchant shipping
is being used for military purposes, it is obvious, that returns
in 1938 from Japan's shipping activities were much less than
during the previous year.
Manufactured goods, as has been
customary, accounted for the greater part of the total exports
but also represented the largest percentage decline from the
figure for l937. Owing largely to a substantial restriction of
the importation of raw meterials, such as raw cotton and wool,
the combined export value of textiles and knitted goods for
1938 greatly decreased from the figure for 1937.
Exporta of
foodstuffs, particularly wheat flour and canned goods, greatly
increased during 1938. The bulk of these shipments was
sent to
"yen bloc" areas.
. The value of the combined exports of
cotton
textiles and raw silk renresented almost 25 per cent
the
total exports for the year.
Total exports for 1938 were said to be Y2,896,707,000
and imports Y2,836,209,000.
This was said to represent a decline
in exports of 12.7 per cent and in imports of 28.3 per cent
compared with 1937.
(b)

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.98)
-22-
(b)
General Economic Conditions.
Toward the latter pert of Decenber it was announced that
the Centrel Price Control Commission had adopted a resolution
emphasizing the importance of eipunding the scope of the present
price control system. With the continuance and further expansion
of economic control measures, it is to be expected that furtlher
efforts will be made to control commodity prices. However, no
immediete steps were taken following the passage of this resolu-
tion.
The "link system" has continued to be a problem involving
controversies between the Governnent and those engaged in foreign
trade. On December 19 it was announced that this system was to
be revised and thnt the period during which cotton goods could be
exported was to bo extended.
Hitherto all persons who handled
cotton goods prior to their export were responsible for the ship-
ment of those goods out of the country. The person responsible
for the export of cotton goods was to be the person who actually
had possession of the goods. Thure was a later announcement of a
special "link system" to be onforccd after January 10, 1939 for
some 24 commodities. Details of this special system are not avail.
able. It is expected, however, that the general proccdure toward
obtaining permits will be simplified and that the maximum time
linits for compulsory export will be extended and various periods
established for different conmodities. The special link system is
being adopted in an attempt to handle those commodities which
because of the complicated relations between raw materials and
manufactured goods are not casily subjected to the industrial link
system now in effect.

of December was not marked by any important changes. There was
further talk of the invocation of additional measures of the
national mobilization lcw but tliere was no noticeable reaction
in business circles epart from that reported for last month.
The usual year-in settlements were carried out without any
apperent stringency and business sentiment seemed to be generally
passive.
